People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, nice to meet you too. I came in because... well, it's the people at my apartment. I know they're watching my comings and goings. It's a big brick building, you know, built back in the 70s. My uncle actually used to do bricklaying on places like that. He helped build the old library downtown before they tore it down. Real shame they knocked it down, they had this whole section on old cars I used to look at. 

But anyway... yeah, they're watching me, and they keep slipping notes about me to the front office. I know they are. Everybody keeps telling me, oh, it's just regular notices on the doors, like for the water getting shut off or whatever, but I know what they're really doing. Like this one guy I saw on Tuesday... Tuesday is usually when I go get my groceries because the bus is less crowded, and I like to get those frozen meatloaf dinners, but... yeah, they're definitely slipping notes about me to the office.

Well, I wouldn't say high or energized. I don't really get like that. But depressed... yeah. Most mornings I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling, like the day already went bad before I even get my feet on the floor. It's just this weight. My mattress doesn't help either, it's one of those old spring ones. I bought it at a discount place over on 4th Street, you know the one with the big yellow sign? Well, let's see... it's been a while now. I'd say it's been about a year and a month since I really noticed them watching me and slipping those notes around the office and the apartment. Well, yeah, sometimes people do say that. Like, when I try to explain things, I'll start off talking about the rent, and then somehow I'm talking about the marina, and then the choir robes, and the wires in the walls, and people just look at me like I skipped half the story. It's like they can't keep up. The marina down by the lake, though, that place is really going downhill. Used to be you could rent a little aluminum rowboat for ten bucks for the whole afternoon. My buddy Dale and I used to go out there and fish for crappie. Dale had this tackle box that was older than I am, rusty green metal, and it always smelled like old bait and motor oil. He lost half his index finger in a table saw accident back in the eighties, but he could still tie a fishing knot faster than anyone I ever met. He moved down to Florida a few years back, lives in one of those retirement parks with the manufactured homes. Says the humidity is terrible for his arthritis, but he likes being near his grandkids. His oldest grandson plays the tuba in the high school marching band. Those uniforms they wear look so heavy, especially in the heat... but yeah, people do say I get off track and they can't follow what I'm saying sometimes.

Huge things, bigger than your hand, with all this sliced almond on top and just drowning in glaze. The lady who ran it, Mrs. Gable, she was a sweetheart. It's like they can't keep up. The marina down by the lake, though, that place is really going downhill. Used to be you could rent a little aluminum rowboat for ten bucks for the whole afternoon. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Putting thoughts in? Taking them out? No, no, nobody's doing that. That sounds like something out of one of those old science fiction movies. You know, the ones they used to show on channel 11 on Saturday afternoons? "Creature Double Feature," they called it. The host was this guy in a cheap vampire cape who always held a rubber bat. I used to watch those with my brother, Tommy. Tommy was crazy about monsters. He had this whole collection of those plastic models you had to glue together and paint yourself. The Wolfman, Frankenstein, the Mummy. He spent hours getting the paint just right on the Mummy's bandages. Used a little toothpick to get the dirt in the cracks so it looked real. He kept them all on this shelf above his bed, and my mother hated dusting in there because she was afraid she'd knock one over and break it. Tommy ended up working at a hardware store out in Ohio. Sells lawnmowers and snowblowers now. He says the winters out there are brutal, not like here. They get lake-effect snow that buries the cars overnight. He had to buy a special heavy-duty shovel just to get to his driveway... but no, I don't have anybody messing with my thoughts like that. Just my own thoughts in there.

No, nobody's really said anything like that to me. My voice is just my voice, I guess. It's not like I'm trying to be an announcer or anything. I used to love listening to those old radio announcers, though. Late at night, you'd get those AM stations coming in from hundreds of miles away, guys with those really deep, smooth voices. I had this silver Panasonic radio with a telescoping antenna that got bent when my buddy Phil sat on it. Phil was a big guy, worked down at the freight yards. He used to bring home these heavy wooden pallets and break them down for firewood. We'd sit out back and have a fire, maybe roast some hot dogs.     "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, they don't have special devices or anything crazy like that, but I know they're watching me. The people at my apartment building, I mean. I came in because I know they're keeping track of my comings and goings, always peeking out when I walk down the hall. And they're slipping notes about me to the front office. Everybody keeps telling me it's just regular notices they tape to the doors, like for water shut-offs or pest control. They had the exterminator come out last month because the lady in 4B said she saw a roach. Big water bugs, really, not the German kind. The German ones are the ones that really infest a place, get into the appliances and everything. My uncle Ray had an apartment in Chicago back in the seventies that got so bad he had to throw out his toaster. He said you'd turn it on and it would smell like burnt hair. He ended up moving out to the suburbs after that, bought a little brick ranch house. Had a nice yard, too, planted a bunch of tomato plants along the fence. Beefsteaks, I think they were. You slice one of those thick, put a little salt and pepper on it, maybe some mayonnaise on white bread... best sandwich in the world. But yeah, those notes on the doors, I know they're really about me, no matter what anyone says.

Dad worked at the bottling plant for thirty years, stamping caps. He used to bring home these glass bottles that had misprints on the labels... but no, my head is fine. I'd say it's been about a year and a month since I really noticed them watching me and slipping those notes around the office and the apartment. A year and a month. That would mean it started right around the time the old bakery on the corner closed down. You know, they used to make these incredible bear claws. Huge things, bigger than your hand, with all this sliced almond on top and just drowning in glaze. The lady who ran it, Mrs. Gable, she was a sweetheart. Always gave me an extra one if I came in right before closing. She had this little poodle that used to sit behind the counter, wearing a little red sweater in the winter. The dog's name was Barnaby. But then her rent went up, and she had to pack it all in. Now it's one of those fancy coffee shops where they charge you five dollars for a cup of black coffee, and the chairs are all made of metal so you can't even sit comfortably. I went in there once and asked for a bear claw, and the kid behind the counter looked at me like I had two heads. He had this little mustache, you know, one of those ones that twirls up at the ends... but yeah, about a year and a month since I started noticing the neighbors doing all that.\n\nDoctor: Do people ever tell you that your speech is hard to follow or that you go off track when you talk?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, sometimes people do say that. Like, when I try to explain things, I'll start off talking about the rent, and then somehow I'm talking about the marina, and then the choir robes, and the wires in the walls, and people just look at me like I skipped half the story. It's like they can't keep up. The marina down by the lake, though, that place is really going downhill. Used to be you could rent a little aluminum rowboat for ten bucks for the whole afternoon. My buddy Dale and I used to go out there and fish for crappie. Dale had this tackle box that was older than I am, rusty green metal, and it always smelled like old bait and motor oil. He lost half his index finger in a table saw accident back in the eighties, but he could still tie a fishing knot faster than anyone I ever met. I used to watch those with my brother, Tommy. Tommy was crazy about monsters. He had this whole collection of those plastic models you had to glue together and paint yourself. I had this silver Panasonic radio with a telescoping antenna that got bent when my buddy Phil sat on it. Phil was a big guy, worked down at the freight yards. And they're slipping notes about me to the front office. Everybody keeps telling me it's just regular notices they tape to the doors, like for water shut-offs or pest control. They had the exterminator come out last month because the lady in 4B said she saw a roach. Big water bugs, really, not the German kind. The German ones are the ones that really infest a place, get into the appliances and everything. My uncle Ray had an apartment in Chicago back in the seventies that got so bad he had to throw out his toaster. He said you'd turn it on and it would smell like burnt hair. He ended up moving out to the suburbs after that, bought a little brick ranch house. Had a nice yard, too, planted a bunch of tomato plants along the fence. Beefsteaks, I think they were. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Energetic? No, not really. I'm usually pretty tired, honestly. Never had a time where I didn't need sleep. Sleep's important, you know? My grandfather always used to say that an hour of sleep before midnight is worth two after. He worked the third shift at the bottling plant for thirty years, so he knew all about messing up his sleep schedule. He'd come home right when the sun was coming up, and my grandmother would have to pull these heavy, dark green curtains shut so he could get some rest. They were made of this really thick velvet material. She bought them at a discount store that used to be downtown, right next to the old movie theater. The theater got torn down back in the eighties to make room for a parking garage. It was a shame, too, because it had this beautiful marquee with all these flashing lightbulbs. They don't make signs like that anymore. Everything's digital now, just big screens everywhere you look. I saw a billboard the other day that changed pictures every ten seconds. It was advertising a car dealership, then a lawyer, then some kind of new energy drink. Those energy drinks can't be good for your heart, all that caffeine and sugar. I stick to coffee, mostly. Just black coffee. But anyway, no, I've never felt like I had too much energy or didn't need to sleep. I just feel worn out most of the time.

Huge things, bigger than your hand, with all this sliced almond on top and just drowning in glaze. The lady who ran it, Mrs. Gable, she was a sweetheart. Always gave me an extra one if I came in right before closing. She had this little poodle that used to sit behind the counter, wearing a little red sweater in the winter. The dog's name was Barnaby. But then her rent went up, and she had to pack it all in. Now it's one of those fancy coffee shops where they charge you five dollars for a cup of black coffee, and the chairs are all made of metal so you can't even sit comfortably. I went in there once and asked for a bear claw, and the kid behind the counter looked at me like I had two heads. He had this little mustache, you know, one of those ones that twirls up at the ends... but yeah, about a year and a month since I started noticing the neighbors doing all that.\n\nDoctor: Do people ever tell you that your speech is hard to follow or that you go off track when you talk?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, sometimes people do say that. He moved down to Florida a few years back, lives in one of those retirement parks with the manufactured homes. Says the humidity is terrible for his arthritis, but he likes being near his grandkids. The guy who runs it, Mr. Patel, he's always inside drinking tea out of this little thermos. It's one of those old plaid thermoses, the kind my dad used to take to the factory. Dad worked at the bottling plant for thirty years, stamping caps. He used to bring home these glass bottles that had misprints on the labels... but no, my head is fine. No medical problems like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices when no one else is around?\n\nPatient: Voices? No, I don't... I don't hear anything like that. Just the usual sounds around the apartment, you know? The radiator clanking. That thing makes a racket every time the heat kicks on. Sounds like somebody hitting a pipe with a wrench. My landlord, Gary, he keeps saying he's gonna bring a guy in to bleed the valves, but he never does. Gary's always too busy fixing up that old Chevy Nova he's got parked in the back alley. It's a '72, I think. Painted this awful mustard yellow color. He says he's gonna take it to car shows when it's done, but it's been sitting on blocks since before the pandemic. I told him he ought to just sell it for scrap, but he got real defensive about it. He's a sensitive guy, Gary. But no, no voices. Just the radiator and Gary's car radio when he's out there tinkering.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been convinced that your neighbors are watching you and sending notes about you?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... it's been a while now. I'd say it's been about a year and a month since I really noticed them watching me and slipping those notes around the office and the apartment. A year and a month. That would mean it started right around the time the old bakery on the corner closed down. You know, they used to make these incredible bear claws. Huge things, bigger than your hand, with all this sliced almond on top and just drowning in glaze. The lady who ran it, Mrs. Gable, she was a sweetheart. Always gave me an extra one if I came in right before closing. She had this little poodle that used to sit behind the counter, wearing a little red sweater in the winter. The dog's name was Barnaby. But then her rent went up, and she had to pack it all in. Now it's one of those fancy coffee shops where they charge you five dollars for a cup of black coffee, and the chairs are all made of metal so you can't even sit comfortably. I went in there once and asked for a bear claw, and the kid behind the counter looked at me like I had two heads. He had this little mustache, you know, one of those ones that twirls up at the ends... but yeah, about a year and a month since I started noticing the neighbors doing all that.\n\nDoctor: Do people ever tell you that your speech is hard to follow or that you go off track when you talk?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, sometimes people do say that. Like, when I try to explain things, I'll start off talking about the rent, and then somehow I'm talking about the marina, and then the choir robes, and the wires in the walls, and people just look at me like I skipped half the story. It's like they can't keep up. The marina down by the lake, though, that place is really going downhill. Used to be you could rent a little aluminum rowboat for ten bucks for the whole afternoon. My buddy Dale and I used to go out there and fish for crappie. Dale had this tackle box that was older than I am, rusty green metal, and it always smelled like old bait and motor oil. He lost half his index finger in a table saw accident back in the eighties, but he could still tie a fishing knot faster than anyone I ever met. He moved down to Florida a few years back, lives in one of those retirement parks with the manufactured homes. Says the humidity is terrible for his arthritis, but he likes being near his grandkids. His oldest grandson plays the tuba in the high school marching band. Those uniforms they wear look so heavy, especially in the heat... but yeah, people do say I get off track and they can't follow what I'm saying sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe anyone is putting thoughts into your mind or taking thoughts out of your mind?\n\nPatient: Putting thoughts in? Taking them out? No, no, nobody's doing that. That sounds like something out of one of those old science fiction movies. You know, the ones they used to show on channel 11 on Saturday afternoons? \"Creature Double Feature,\" they called it. The host was this guy in a cheap vampire cape who always held a rubber bat. I used to watch those with my brother, Tommy. Tommy was crazy about monsters. He had this whole collection of those plastic models you had to glue together and paint yourself. The Wolfman, Frankenstein, the Mummy. He spent hours getting the paint just right on the Mummy's bandages. Used a little toothpick to get the dirt in the cracks so it looked real. He kept them all on this shelf above his bed, and my mother hated dusting in there because she was afraid she'd knock one over and break it. Tommy ended up working at a hardware store out in Ohio. Sells lawnmowers and snowblowers now. He says the winters out there are brutal, not like here. They get lake-effect snow that buries the cars overnight. He had to buy a special heavy-duty shovel just to get to his driveway... but no, I don't have anybody messing with my thoughts like that. Just my own thoughts in there.\n\nDoctor: Have people told you that your face or voice seems less expressive than it used to be?\n\nPatient: No, nobody's really said anything like that to me. My voice is just my voice, I guess. It's not like I'm trying to be an announcer or anything. I used to love listening to those old radio announcers, though. Late at night, you'd get those AM stations coming in from hundreds of miles away, guys with those really deep, smooth voices. I had this silver Panasonic radio with a telescoping antenna that got bent when my buddy Phil sat on it. Phil was a big guy, worked down at the freight yards. He used to bring home these heavy wooden pallets and break them down for firewood. We'd sit out back and have a fire, maybe roast some hot dogs. You can't beat a hot dog cooked over real wood, gets that nice char on the outside. We used those cheap buns, though, the ones that fall apart the second you put mustard on them. I always liked the spicy brown mustard, not that bright yellow stuff. The yellow stuff stains your shirts, and I ruined a perfectly good flannel that way once. Had a tear in the elbow anyway, but still... what were we talking about? Oh, right, my face and voice. No, no one's told me I'm less expressive or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your neighbors are able to do anything impossible, like control events from a distance or use special devices to monitor you?\n\nPatient: Well, they don't have special devices or anything crazy like that, but I know they're watching me. The people at my apartment building, I mean. I came in because I know they're keeping track of my comings and goings, always peeking out when I walk down the hall. And they're slipping notes about me to the front office. Everybody keeps telling me it's just regular notices they tape to the doors, like for water shut-offs or pest control. They had the exterminator come out last month because the lady in 4B said she saw a roach. Big water bugs, really, not the German kind. The German ones are the ones that really infest a place, get into the appliances and everything. My uncle Ray had an apartment in Chicago back in the seventies that got so bad he had to throw out his toaster. He said you'd turn it on and it would smell like burnt hair. He ended up moving out to the suburbs after that, bought a little brick ranch house. Had a nice yard, too, planted a bunch of tomato plants along the fence. Beefsteaks, I think they were. You slice one of those thick, put a little salt and pepper on it, maybe some mayonnaise on white bread... best sandwich in the world. But yeah, those notes on the doors, I know they're really about me, no matter what anyone says.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt unusually energetic and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Energetic? No, not really. I'm usually pretty tired, honestly. Never had a time where I didn't need sleep. Sleep's important, you know? My grandfather always used to say that an hour of sleep before midnight is worth two after. He worked the third shift at the bottling plant for thirty years, so he knew all about messing up his sleep schedule. He'd come home right when the sun was coming up, and my grandmother would have to pull these heavy, dark green curtains shut so he could get some rest. They were made of this really thick velvet material. She bought them at a discount store that used to be downtown, right next to the old movie theater. The theater got torn down back in the eighties to make room for a parking garage. It was a shame, too, because it had this beautiful marquee with all these flashing lightbulbs. They don't make signs like that anymore. Everything's digital now, just big screens everywhere you look. I saw a billboard the other day that changed pictures every ten seconds. It was advertising a car dealership, then a lawyer, then some kind of new energy drink. Those energy drinks can't be good for your heart, all that caffeine and sugar. I stick to coffee, mostly. Just black coffee. But anyway, no, I've never felt like I had too much energy or didn't need to sleep. I just feel worn out most of the time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Six months? No, it's been longer than that. It's been about a year and a month now, dealing with them watching me and the notes and everything. I know exactly how long because it started right around the time the transmission went out on my old Buick. Just clunked out right in the middle of the intersection over by the hardware store. 

That hardware store's been there since I was a kid, you know. You go in there and it has that specific smell... like sawdust and fertilizer mixed together. They used to have this old golden retriever that just slept by the front door. You'd have to step right over him to get to the paint aisle. Paint's gotten so expensive lately, too. I was looking at a can of white semi-gloss a while back just to touch up the baseboards, and I couldn't believe the price. Used to be you could get a gallon for a few bucks. My dad was a house painter for a little while, back in the seventies. He always wore these white overalls that were just covered in every color imaginable. He looked like a modern art painting himself by the time he got home. He'd leave his boots on the porch so he wouldn't track it in... 

But, uh, yeah. To answer your question, it's been a year and a month since all this stuff with the neighbors started.

Like this one guy I saw on Tuesday... Tuesday is usually when I go get my groceries because the bus is less crowded, and I like to get those frozen meatloaf dinners, but... yeah, they're definitely slipping notes about me to the office.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had a period when you felt very depressed or unusually high or energized most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say high or energized. I don't really get like that. But depressed... yeah. Most mornings I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling, like the day already went bad before I even get my feet on the floor. It's just this weight. My mattress doesn't help either, it's one of those old spring ones. I bought it at a discount place over on 4th Street, you know the one with the big yellow sign? The guy there told me it was firm, but I think he just wanted to make a sale. He had this little dog behind the counter, a terrier mix, I think. Reminded me of a dog I had when I was a kid, Buster. Buster used to chase the mail truck every single day... but yeah, I just feel so down and empty most days.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when people were watching you and slipping notes about you even when you were not feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually... there was a spell a little while back. It lasted for several weeks, I think. I wasn't waking up feeling so hopeless, and I was actually eating a bit better. I had started making those big pots of chili again. My sister taught me how to make it—she puts just a little bit of cocoa powder in the pot. Sounds strange, I know, but it really deepens the flavor. She moved out to Ohio back in '08, right near Cleveland. I took a Greyhound bus out there once to visit, and let me tell you, those buses are not built for comfort. My knees were aching for days. The driver was this older fella who kept playing old Willie Nelson tapes the whole way... \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right. Even during those weeks when my mood lifted and I was doing okay, I still knew the neighbors were watching my door. I still knew they were reporting on me. Feeling better didn't change the fact that they were out there doing that.\n\nDoctor: Before all this started, did you have any major medical illness or head injury?\n\nPatient: No, no major medical stuff, and I haven't bumped my head or anything like that. Well, I mean, I did scrape my elbow pretty bad last winter when I slipped on the ice outside the corner store. They never shovel that sidewalk right. The guy who runs it, Mr. Patel, he's always inside drinking tea out of this little thermos. It's one of those old plaid thermoses, the kind my dad used to take to the factory. Dad worked at the bottling plant for thirty years, stamping caps. He used to bring home these glass bottles that had misprints on the labels... but no, my head is fine. No medical problems like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices when no one else is around?\n\nPatient: Voices? No, I don't... I don't hear anything like that. Just the usual sounds around the apartment, you know? The radiator clanking. That thing makes a racket every time the heat kicks on. Sounds like somebody hitting a pipe with a wrench. My landlord, Gary, he keeps saying he's gonna bring a guy in to bleed the valves, but he never does. Gary's always too busy fixing up that old Chevy Nova he's got parked in the back alley. It's a '72, I think. Painted this awful mustard yellow color. He says he's gonna take it to car shows when it's done, but it's been sitting on blocks since before the pandemic. I told him he ought to just sell it for scrap, but he got real defensive about it. He's a sensitive guy, Gary. But no, no voices. Just the radiator and Gary's car radio when he's out there tinkering.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been convinced that your neighbors are watching you and sending notes about you?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... it's been a while now. I'd say it's been about a year and a month since I really noticed them watching me and slipping those notes around the office and the apartment. A year and a month. That would mean it started right around the time the old bakery on the corner closed down. You know, they used to make these incredible bear claws. Huge things, bigger than your hand, with all this sliced almond on top and just drowning in glaze. The lady who ran it, Mrs. Gable, she was a sweetheart. Always gave me an extra one if I came in right before closing. She had this little poodle that used to sit behind the counter, wearing a little red sweater in the winter. The dog's name was Barnaby. But then her rent went up, and she had to pack it all in. Now it's one of those fancy coffee shops where they charge you five dollars for a cup of black coffee, and the chairs are all made of metal so you can't even sit comfortably. I went in there once and asked for a bear claw, and the kid behind the counter looked at me like I had two heads. He had this little mustache, you know, one of those ones that twirls up at the ends... but yeah, about a year and a month since I started noticing the neighbors doing all that.\n\nDoctor: Do people ever tell you that your speech is hard to follow or that you go off track when you talk?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, sometimes people do say that. Like, when I try to explain things, I'll start off talking about the rent, and then somehow I'm talking about the marina, and then the choir robes, and the wires in the walls, and people just look at me like I skipped half the story. It's like they can't keep up. The marina down by the lake, though, that place is really going downhill. Used to be you could rent a little aluminum rowboat for ten bucks for the whole afternoon. My buddy Dale and I used to go out there and fish for crappie. Dale had this tackle box that was older than I am, rusty green metal, and it always smelled like old bait and motor oil. He lost half his index finger in a table saw accident back in the eighties, but he could still tie a fishing knot faster than anyone I ever met. He moved down to Florida a few years back, lives in one of those retirement parks with the manufactured homes. Says the humidity is terrible for his arthritis, but he likes being near his grandkids. His oldest grandson plays the tuba in the high school marching band. Those uniforms they wear look so heavy, especially in the heat... but yeah, people do say I get off track and they can't follow what I'm saying sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe anyone is putting thoughts into your mind or taking thoughts out of your mind?\n\nPatient: Putting thoughts in? Taking them out? No, no, nobody's doing that. That sounds like something out of one of those old science fiction movies. You know, the ones they used to show on channel 11 on Saturday afternoons? \"Creature Double Feature,\" they called it. The host was this guy in a cheap vampire cape who always held a rubber bat. I used to watch those with my brother, Tommy. Tommy was crazy about monsters. He had this whole collection of those plastic models you had to glue together and paint yourself. The Wolfman, Frankenstein, the Mummy. He spent hours getting the paint just right on the Mummy's bandages. Used a little toothpick to get the dirt in the cracks so it looked real. He kept them all on this shelf above his bed, and my mother hated dusting in there because she was afraid she'd knock one over and break it. Tommy ended up working at a hardware store out in Ohio. Sells lawnmowers and snowblowers now. He says the winters out there are brutal, not like here. They get lake-effect snow that buries the cars overnight. He had to buy a special heavy-duty shovel just to get to his driveway... but no, I don't have anybody messing with my thoughts like that. Just my own thoughts in there.\n\nDoctor: Have people told you that your face or voice seems less expressive than it used to be?\n\nPatient: No, nobody's really said anything like that to me. My voice is just my voice, I guess. It's not like I'm trying to be an announcer or anything. I used to love listening to those old radio announcers, though. Late at night, you'd get those AM stations coming in from hundreds of miles away, guys with those really deep, smooth voices. I had this silver Panasonic radio with a telescoping antenna that got bent when my buddy Phil sat on it. Phil was a big guy, worked down at the freight yards. He used to bring home these heavy wooden pallets and break them down for firewood. We'd sit out back and have a fire, maybe roast some hot dogs. You can't beat a hot dog cooked over real wood, gets that nice char on the outside. We used those cheap buns, though, the ones that fall apart the second you put mustard on them. I always liked the spicy brown mustard, not that bright yellow stuff. The yellow stuff stains your shirts, and I ruined a perfectly good flannel that way once. Had a tear in the elbow anyway, but still... what were we talking about? Oh, right, my face and voice. No, no one's told me I'm less expressive or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your neighbors are able to do anything impossible, like control events from a distance or use special devices to monitor you?\n\nPatient: Well, they don't have special devices or anything crazy like that, but I know they're watching me. The people at my apartment building, I mean. I came in because I know they're keeping track of my comings and goings, always peeking out when I walk down the hall. And they're slipping notes about me to the front office. Everybody keeps telling me it's just regular notices they tape to the doors, like for water shut-offs or pest control. They had the exterminator come out last month because the lady in 4B said she saw a roach. Big water bugs, really, not the German kind. The German ones are the ones that really infest a place, get into the appliances and everything. My uncle Ray had an apartment in Chicago back in the seventies that got so bad he had to throw out his toaster. He said you'd turn it on and it would smell like burnt hair. He ended up moving out to the suburbs after that, bought a little brick ranch house. Had a nice yard, too, planted a bunch of tomato plants along the fence. Beefsteaks, I think they were. You slice one of those thick, put a little salt and pepper on it, maybe some mayonnaise on white bread... best sandwich in the world. But yeah, those notes on the doors, I know they're really about me, no matter what anyone says.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt unusually energetic and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Energetic? No, not really. I'm usually pretty tired, honestly. Never had a time where I didn't need sleep. Sleep's important, you know? My grandfather always used to say that an hour of sleep before midnight is worth two after. He worked the third shift at the bottling plant for thirty years, so he knew all about messing up his sleep schedule. He'd come home right when the sun was coming up, and my grandmother would have to pull these heavy, dark green curtains shut so he could get some rest. They were made of this really thick velvet material. She bought them at a discount store that used to be downtown, right next to the old movie theater. The theater got torn down back in the eighties to make room for a parking garage. It was a shame, too, because it had this beautiful marquee with all these flashing lightbulbs. They don't make signs like that anymore. Everything's digital now, just big screens everywhere you look. I saw a billboard the other day that changed pictures every ten seconds. It was advertising a car dealership, then a lawyer, then some kind of new energy drink. Those energy drinks can't be good for your heart, all that caffeine and sugar. I stick to coffee, mostly. Just black coffee. But anyway, no, I've never felt like I had too much energy or didn't need to sleep. I just feel worn out most of the time.\n\nDoctor: For at least six months, have you had these problems with people watching you and sending notes about you?\n\nPatient: Six months? No, it's been longer than that. It's been about a year and a month now, dealing with them watching me and the notes and everything. I know exactly how long because it started right around the time the transmission went out on my old Buick. Just clunked out right in the middle of the intersection over by the hardware store. \n\nThat hardware store's been there since I was a kid, you know. You go in there and it has that specific smell... like sawdust and fertilizer mixed together. They used to have this old golden retriever that just slept by the front door. You'd have to step right over him to get to the paint aisle. Paint's gotten so expensive lately, too. I was looking at a can of white semi-gloss a while back just to touch up the baseboards, and I couldn't believe the price. Used to be you could get a gallon for a few bucks. My dad was a house painter for a little while, back in the seventies. He always wore these white overalls that were just covered in every color imaginable. He looked like a modern art painting himself by the time he got home. He'd leave his boots on the porch so he wouldn't track it in... \n\nBut, uh, yeah. To answer your question, it's been a year and a month since all this stuff with the neighbors started.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah, I guess I have. I used to look forward to a lot of things, really. Choir practice on Wednesdays, playing bridge with the guys, even just cooking up a big pot of beans on Sunday... I used to do little repair jobs for folks around the building, too, fixing leaky faucets or patching drywall. But now, I just mostly let the phone ring. I tell myself there's no point in going or doing any of it. 

I used to be real handy, you know. I had this big red toolbox my brother gave me for my birthday one year. It had all these little compartments on top for screws and nails, kept everything sorted. Sorting things used to be real calming for me. I'd sit at the kitchen table and organize all my hardware by size. My brother, he moved out to Arizona about ten years ago. Said he couldn't take the winters here anymore. He bought a house out in the desert, said he doesn't even have to mow the lawn because it's all rocks and cactus. Sounds kind of nice, not having to worry about yard work. Mowers can be a real hassle when the carburetor gets gunked up. You gotta take the whole thing apart and soak it in carburetor cleaner... smells awful, that stuff. Gives you a headache if you don't do it in a well-ventilated area. But yeah, I just don't have the energy or the care for any of my old hobbies anymore. Just seems pointless.

My grandfather always used to say that an hour of sleep before midnight is worth two after. He worked the third shift at the bottling plant for thirty years, so he knew all about messing up his sleep schedule. He'd come home right when the sun was coming up, and my grandmother would have to pull these heavy, dark green curtains shut so he could get some rest. They were made of this really thick velvet material. She bought them at a discount store that used to be downtown, right next to the old movie theater. The theater got torn down back in the eighties to make room for a parking garage. It was a shame, too, because it had this beautiful marquee with all these flashing lightbulbs. They don't make signs like that anymore. Everything's digital now, just big screens everywhere you look. I saw a billboard the other day that changed pictures every ten seconds. It was advertising a car dealership, then a lawyer, then some kind of new energy drink. Those energy drinks can't be good for your heart, all that caffeine and sugar. I stick to coffee, mostly. Just black coffee. But anyway, no, I've never felt like I had too much energy or didn't need to sleep. I just feel worn out most of the time.\n\nDoctor: For at least six months, have you had these problems with people watching you and sending notes about you?\n\nPatient: Six months? No, it's been longer than that. It's been about a year and a month now, dealing with them watching me and the notes and everything. I know exactly how long because it started right around the time the transmission went out on my old Buick. Just clunked out right in the middle of the intersection over by the hardware store. \n\nThat hardware store's been there since I was a kid, you know. You go in there and it has that specific smell... like sawdust and fertilizer mixed together. They used to have this old golden retriever that just slept by the front door. You'd have to step right over him to get to the paint aisle. Paint's gotten so expensive lately, too. I was looking at a can of white semi-gloss a while back just to touch up the baseboards, and I couldn't believe the price. Used to be you could get a gallon for a few bucks. My dad was a house painter for a little while, back in the seventies. He always wore these white overalls that were just covered in every color imaginable. He looked like a modern art painting himself by the time he got home. He'd leave his boots on the porch so he wouldn't track it in... \n\nBut, uh, yeah. To answer your question, it's been a year and a month since all this stuff with the neighbors started.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I have. I used to look forward to a lot of things, really. Choir practice on Wednesdays, playing bridge with the guys, even just cooking up a big pot of beans on Sunday... I used to do little repair jobs for folks around the building, too, fixing leaky faucets or patching drywall. But now, I just mostly let the phone ring. I tell myself there's no point in going or doing any of it. \n\nI used to be real handy, you know. I had this big red toolbox my brother gave me for my birthday one year. It had all these little compartments on top for screws and nails, kept everything sorted. Sorting things used to be real calming for me. I'd sit at the kitchen table and organize all my hardware by size. My brother, he moved out to Arizona about ten years ago. Said he couldn't take the winters here anymore. He bought a house out in the desert, said he doesn't even have to mow the lawn because it's all rocks and cactus. Sounds kind of nice, not having to worry about yard work. Mowers can be a real hassle when the carburetor gets gunked up. You gotta take the whole thing apart and soak it in carburetor cleaner... smells awful, that stuff. Gives you a headache if you don't do it in a well-ventilated area. But yeah, I just don't have the energy or the care for any of my old hobbies anymore. Just seems pointless.\n\n[Last Question]\nWas there ever a period of at least two weeks when you still believed your neighbors were watching you and sending notes about you, but you were not feeling depressed at all?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 59 / not_in_workforce / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 3\nmanifestation: \"There was a spell of several weeks when I was eating a bit better and not waking up so hopeless, but I still believed the neighbors were watching my door and reporting on me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, there was. There was a spell of several weeks, maybe a month or so ago, where I was actually eating a bit better. Woke up not feeling quite so... heavy and hopeless, you know? But I still knew they were watching my door. Still knew they were reporting on me. 

Speaking of doors, my front door has this squeak that just won't quit. It's the bottom hinge. I've tried WD-40, I've tried rubbing a bar of soap on it—my mom used to swear by that trick for squeaky floorboards. She had this old house with these original hardwood floors that groaned every time you took a step. It was impossible to sneak down to the kitchen for a midnight snack when I was a kid. She'd always catch me. She'd be sitting there in the dark, drinking a glass of warm milk. Said it helped her sleep. I never understood how people could drink warm milk, it just coats your throat in this weird way. But she liked it. She always had it in this chipped blue mug with a little white sailboat painted on it. I wonder whatever happened to that mug... probably got lost in the shuffle when we cleaned out her house. It's funny the things you hold onto and the things that just slip away. But yeah, even when I was feeling a bit more normal, eating alright and not so down, I still knew they had their eyes on me and were sending those notes.

Just the radiator and Gary's car radio when he's out there tinkering.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been convinced that your neighbors are watching you and sending notes about you?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... it's been a while now. I'd say it's been about a year and a month since I really noticed them watching me and slipping those notes around the office and the apartment. A year and a month. That would mean it started right around the time the old bakery on the corner closed down. You know, they used to make these incredible bear claws. Huge things, bigger than your hand, with all this sliced almond on top and just drowning in glaze. The lady who ran it, Mrs. Gable, she was a sweetheart. Always gave me an extra one if I came in right before closing. She had this little poodle that used to sit behind the counter, wearing a little red sweater in the winter. The dog's name was Barnaby. But then her rent went up, and she had to pack it all in. Now it's one of those fancy coffee shops where they charge you five dollars for a cup of black coffee, and the chairs are all made of metal so you can't even sit comfortably. I went in there once and asked for a bear claw, and the kid behind the counter looked at me like I had two heads. He had this little mustache, you know, one of those ones that twirls up at the ends... but yeah, about a year and a month since I started noticing the neighbors doing all that.\n\nDoctor: Do people ever tell you that your speech is hard to follow or that you go off track when you talk?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, sometimes people do say that. Like, when I try to explain things, I'll start off talking about the rent, and then somehow I'm talking about the marina, and then the choir robes, and the wires in the walls, and people just look at me like I skipped half the story. It's like they can't keep up. The marina down by the lake, though, that place is really going downhill. Used to be you could rent a little aluminum rowboat for ten bucks for the whole afternoon. My buddy Dale and I used to go out there and fish for crappie. Dale had this tackle box that was older than I am, rusty green metal, and it always smelled like old bait and motor oil. He lost half his index finger in a table saw accident back in the eighties, but he could still tie a fishing knot faster than anyone I ever met. He moved down to Florida a few years back, lives in one of those retirement parks with the manufactured homes. Says the humidity is terrible for his arthritis, but he likes being near his grandkids. His oldest grandson plays the tuba in the high school marching band. Those uniforms they wear look so heavy, especially in the heat... but yeah, people do say I get off track and they can't follow what I'm saying sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe anyone is putting thoughts into your mind or taking thoughts out of your mind?\n\nPatient: Putting thoughts in? Taking them out? No, no, nobody's doing that. That sounds like something out of one of those old science fiction movies. You know, the ones they used to show on channel 11 on Saturday afternoons? \"Creature Double Feature,\" they called it. The host was this guy in a cheap vampire cape who always held a rubber bat. I used to watch those with my brother, Tommy. Tommy was crazy about monsters. He had this whole collection of those plastic models you had to glue together and paint yourself. The Wolfman, Frankenstein, the Mummy. He spent hours getting the paint just right on the Mummy's bandages. Used a little toothpick to get the dirt in the cracks so it looked real. He kept them all on this shelf above his bed, and my mother hated dusting in there because she was afraid she'd knock one over and break it. Tommy ended up working at a hardware store out in Ohio. Sells lawnmowers and snowblowers now. He says the winters out there are brutal, not like here. They get lake-effect snow that buries the cars overnight. He had to buy a special heavy-duty shovel just to get to his driveway... but no, I don't have anybody messing with my thoughts like that. Just my own thoughts in there.\n\nDoctor: Have people told you that your face or voice seems less expressive than it used to be?\n\nPatient: No, nobody's really said anything like that to me. My voice is just my voice, I guess. It's not like I'm trying to be an announcer or anything. I used to love listening to those old radio announcers, though. Late at night, you'd get those AM stations coming in from hundreds of miles away, guys with those really deep, smooth voices. I had this silver Panasonic radio with a telescoping antenna that got bent when my buddy Phil sat on it. Phil was a big guy, worked down at the freight yards. He used to bring home these heavy wooden pallets and break them down for firewood. We'd sit out back and have a fire, maybe roast some hot dogs. You can't beat a hot dog cooked over real wood, gets that nice char on the outside. We used those cheap buns, though, the ones that fall apart the second you put mustard on them. I always liked the spicy brown mustard, not that bright yellow stuff. The yellow stuff stains your shirts, and I ruined a perfectly good flannel that way once. Had a tear in the elbow anyway, but still... what were we talking about? Oh, right, my face and voice. No, no one's told me I'm less expressive or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your neighbors are able to do anything impossible, like control events from a distance or use special devices to monitor you?\n\nPatient: Well, they don't have special devices or anything crazy like that, but I know they're watching me. The people at my apartment building, I mean. I came in because I know they're keeping track of my comings and goings, always peeking out when I walk down the hall. And they're slipping notes about me to the front office. Everybody keeps telling me it's just regular notices they tape to the doors, like for water shut-offs or pest control. They had the exterminator come out last month because the lady in 4B said she saw a roach. Big water bugs, really, not the German kind. The German ones are the ones that really infest a place, get into the appliances and everything. My uncle Ray had an apartment in Chicago back in the seventies that got so bad he had to throw out his toaster. He said you'd turn it on and it would smell like burnt hair. He ended up moving out to the suburbs after that, bought a little brick ranch house. Had a nice yard, too, planted a bunch of tomato plants along the fence. Beefsteaks, I think they were. You slice one of those thick, put a little salt and pepper on it, maybe some mayonnaise on white bread... best sandwich in the world. You go in there and it has that specific smell... like sawdust and fertilizer mixed together. They used to have this old golden retriever that just slept by the front door. You'd have to step right over him to get to the paint aisle. Paint's gotten so expensive lately, too. I was looking at a can of white semi-gloss a while back just to touch up the baseboards, and I couldn't believe the price. Used to be you could get a gallon for a few bucks. Just the radiator and Gary's car radio when he's out there tinkering.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been convinced that your neighbors are watching you and sending notes about you?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... it's been a while now. I'd say it's been about a year and a month since I really noticed them watching me and slipping those notes around the office and the apartment. A year and a month. That would mean it started right around the time the old bakery on the corner closed down. You know, they used to make these incredible bear claws. Huge things, bigger than your hand, with all this sliced almond on top and just drowning in glaze. The lady who ran it, Mrs. Gable, she was a sweetheart. Always gave me an extra one if I came in right before closing. She had this little poodle that used to sit behind the counter, wearing a little red sweater in the winter. The dog's name was Barnaby. But then her rent went up, and she had to pack it all in. Now it's one of those fancy coffee shops where they charge you five dollars for a cup of black coffee, and the chairs are all made of metal so you can't even sit comfortably. I went in there once and asked for a bear claw, and the kid behind the counter looked at me like I had two heads. He had this little mustache, you know, one of those ones that twirls up at the ends... but yeah, about a year and a month since I started noticing the neighbors doing all that.\n\nDoctor: Do people ever tell you that your speech is hard to follow or that you go off track when you talk?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, sometimes people do say that. Like, when I try to explain things, I'll start off talking about the rent, and then somehow I'm talking about the marina, and then the choir robes, and the wires in the walls, and people just look at me like I skipped half the story. It's like they can't keep up. The marina down by the lake, though, that place is really going downhill. Used to be you could rent a little aluminum rowboat for ten bucks for the whole afternoon. My buddy Dale and I used to go out there and fish for crappie. Dale had this tackle box that was older than I am, rusty green metal, and it always smelled like old bait and motor oil. He lost half his index finger in a table saw accident back in the eighties, but he could still tie a fishing knot faster than anyone I ever met. He moved down to Florida a few years back, lives in one of those retirement parks with the manufactured homes. Says the humidity is terrible for his arthritis, but he likes being near his grandkids. His oldest grandson plays the tuba in the high school marching band. Those uniforms they wear look so heavy, especially in the heat... but yeah, people do say I get off track and they can't follow what I'm saying sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe anyone is putting thoughts into your mind or taking thoughts out of your mind?\n\nPatient: Putting thoughts in? Taking them out? No, no, nobody's doing that. That sounds like something out of one of those old science fiction movies. You know, the ones they used to show on channel 11 on Saturday afternoons? \"Creature Double Feature,\" they called it. The host was this guy in a cheap vampire cape who always held a rubber bat. I used to watch those with my brother, Tommy. Tommy was crazy about monsters. He had this whole collection of those plastic models you had to glue together and paint yourself. The Wolfman, Frankenstein, the Mummy. He spent hours getting the paint just right on the Mummy's bandages. Used a little toothpick to get the dirt in the cracks so it looked real. He kept them all on this shelf above his bed, and my mother hated dusting in there because she was afraid she'd knock one over and break it. Tommy ended up working at a hardware store out in Ohio. Sells lawnmowers and snowblowers now. He says the winters out there are brutal, not like here. They get lake-effect snow that buries the cars overnight. He had to buy a special heavy-duty shovel just to get to his driveway... but no, I don't have anybody messing with my thoughts like that. Just my own thoughts in there.\n\nDoctor: Have people told you that your face or voice seems less expressive than it used to be?\n\nPatient: No, nobody's really said anything like that to me. My voice is just my voice, I guess. It's not like I'm trying to be an announcer or anything. I used to love listening to those old radio announcers, though. Late at night, you'd get those AM stations coming in from hundreds of miles away, guys with those really deep, smooth voices. I had this silver Panasonic radio with a telescoping antenna that got bent when my buddy Phil sat on it. Phil was a big guy, worked down at the freight yards. He used to bring home these heavy wooden pallets and break them down for firewood. We'd sit out back and have a fire, maybe roast some hot dogs. You can't beat a hot dog cooked over real wood, gets that nice char on the outside. We used those cheap buns, though, the ones that fall apart the second you put mustard on them. I always liked the spicy brown mustard, not that bright yellow stuff. The yellow stuff stains your shirts, and I ruined a perfectly good flannel that way once. Had a tear in the elbow anyway, but still... what were we talking about? Oh, right, my face and voice. No, no one's told me I'm less expressive or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your neighbors are able to do anything impossible, like control events from a distance or use special devices to monitor you?\n\nPatient: Well, they don't have special devices or anything crazy like that, but I know they're watching me. The people at my apartment building, I mean. I came in because I know they're keeping track of my comings and goings, always peeking out when I walk down the hall. And they're slipping notes about me to the front office. Everybody keeps telling me it's just regular notices they tape to the doors, like for water shut-offs or pest control. They had the exterminator come out last month because the lady in 4B said she saw a roach. Big water bugs, really, not the German kind. The German ones are the ones that really infest a place, get into the appliances and everything. My uncle Ray had an apartment in Chicago back in the seventies that got so bad he had to throw out his toaster. He said you'd turn it on and it would smell like burnt hair. He ended up moving out to the suburbs after that, bought a little brick ranch house. Had a nice yard, too, planted a bunch of tomato plants along the fence. Beefsteaks, I think they were. You slice one of those thick, put a little salt and pepper on it, maybe some mayonnaise on white bread... best sandwich in the world. But yeah, those notes on the doors, I know they're really about me, no matter what anyone says.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt unusually energetic and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Energetic? No, not really. I'm usually pretty tired, honestly. Never had a time where I didn't need sleep. Sleep's important, you know? My grandfather always used to say that an hour of sleep before midnight is worth two after. He worked the third shift at the bottling plant for thirty years, so he knew all about messing up his sleep schedule. He'd come home right when the sun was coming up, and my grandmother would have to pull these heavy, dark green curtains shut so he could get some rest. They were made of this really thick velvet material. She bought them at a discount store that used to be downtown, right next to the old movie theater. The theater got torn down back in the eighties to make room for a parking garage. It was a shame, too, because it had this beautiful marquee with all these flashing lightbulbs. They don't make signs like that anymore. Everything's digital now, just big screens everywhere you look. I saw a billboard the other day that changed pictures every ten seconds. It was advertising a car dealership, then a lawyer, then some kind of new energy drink. Those energy drinks can't be good for your heart, all that caffeine and sugar. I stick to coffee, mostly. Just black coffee. But anyway, no, I've never felt like I had too much energy or didn't need to sleep. I just feel worn out most of the time.\n\nDoctor: For at least six months, have you had these problems with people watching you and sending notes about you?\n\nPatient: Six months? No, it's been longer than that. It's been about a year and a month now, dealing with them watching me and the notes and everything. I know exactly how long because it started right around the time the transmission went out on my old Buick. Just clunked out right in the middle of the intersection over by the hardware store. \n\nThat hardware store's been there since I was a kid, you know. You go in there and it has that specific smell... like sawdust and fertilizer mixed together. They used to have this old golden retriever that just slept by the front door. You'd have to step right over him to get to the paint aisle. Paint's gotten so expensive lately, too. I was looking at a can of white semi-gloss a while back just to touch up the baseboards, and I couldn't believe the price. Used to be you could get a gallon for a few bucks. My dad was a house painter for a little while, back in the seventies. He always wore these white overalls that were just covered in every color imaginable. He looked like a modern art painting himself by the time he got home. He'd leave his boots on the porch so he wouldn't track it in... \n\nBut, uh, yeah. To answer your question, it's been a year and a month since all this stuff with the neighbors started.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I have. I used to look forward to a lot of things, really. Choir practice on Wednesdays, playing bridge with the guys, even just cooking up a big pot of beans on Sunday... I used to do little repair jobs for folks around the building, too, fixing leaky faucets or patching drywall. But now, I just mostly let the phone ring. I tell myself there's no point in going or doing any of it. \n\nI used to be real handy, you know. I had this big red toolbox my brother gave me for my birthday one year. It had all these little compartments on top for screws and nails, kept everything sorted. Sorting things used to be real calming for me. I'd sit at the kitchen table and organize all my hardware by size. My brother, he moved out to Arizona about ten years ago. Said he couldn't take the winters here anymore. He bought a house out in the desert, said he doesn't even have to mow the lawn because it's all rocks and cactus. Sounds kind of nice, not having to worry about yard work. Mowers can be a real hassle when the carburetor gets gunked up. You gotta take the whole thing apart and soak it in carburetor cleaner... smells awful, that stuff. Gives you a headache if you don't do it in a well-ventilated area. But yeah, I just don't have the energy or the care for any of my old hobbies anymore. Just seems pointless.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you still believed your neighbors were watching you and sending notes about you, but you were not feeling depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Yeah, there was. There was a spell of several weeks, maybe a month or so ago, where I was actually eating a bit better. Woke up not feeling quite so... heavy and hopeless, you know? But I still knew they were watching my door. Still knew they were reporting on me. \n\nSpeaking of doors, my front door has this squeak that just won't quit. It's the bottom hinge. I've tried WD-40, I've tried rubbing a bar of soap on it—my mom used to swear by that trick for squeaky floorboards. She had this old house with these original hardwood floors that groaned every time you took a step. It was impossible to sneak down to the kitchen for a midnight snack when I was a kid. She'd always catch me. She'd be sitting there in the dark, drinking a glass of warm milk. Said it helped her sleep. I never understood how people could drink warm milk, it just coats your throat in this weird way. But she liked it. She always had it in this chipped blue mug with a little white sailboat painted on it. I wonder whatever happened to that mug... probably got lost in the shuffle when we cleaned out her house. It's funny the things you hold onto and the things that just slip away. But yeah, even when I was feeling a bit more normal, eating alright and not so down, I still knew they had their eyes on me and were sending those notes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Outside forces? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I'm the one moving my own arms and legs, you know? I do what I decide to do. 

Speaking of legs, though, my left knee has been acting up lately whenever it rains. Just aches right down to the bone. Reminds me of when I fell off the roof of my buddy Al's garage back in '88. We were trying to fix the shingles before a big storm rolled in. Black clouds just sitting over the whole neighborhood, looked like the end of the world. Al had this old Ford pickup truck parked in the driveway, a real beauty, cherry red. He used to spend every Saturday polishing that thing until you could see your reflection in the bumper. Chrome bumpers... they don't make 'em like that anymore. Now everything's just plastic and fiberglass on cars. If you get into a fender bender nowadays, the whole front end just crumbles like a soda can. Used to be you could just take a rubber mallet and pound a dent right out yourself. But yeah, those notes on the doors, I know they're really about me, no matter what anyone says.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt unusually energetic and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Energetic? No, not really. I'm usually pretty tired, honestly. Never had a time where I didn't need sleep. Sleep's important, you know? My grandfather always used to say that an hour of sleep before midnight is worth two after. He worked the third shift at the bottling plant for thirty years, so he knew all about messing up his sleep schedule. He'd come home right when the sun was coming up, and my grandmother would have to pull these heavy, dark green curtains shut so he could get some rest. They were made of this really thick velvet material. She bought them at a discount store that used to be downtown, right next to the old movie theater. The theater got torn down back in the eighties to make room for a parking garage. It was a shame, too, because it had this beautiful marquee with all these flashing lightbulbs. They don't make signs like that anymore. Everything's digital now, just big screens everywhere you look. I saw a billboard the other day that changed pictures every ten seconds. It was advertising a car dealership, then a lawyer, then some kind of new energy drink. Those energy drinks can't be good for your heart, all that caffeine and sugar. I stick to coffee, mostly. Just black coffee. But anyway, no, I've never felt like I had too much energy or didn't need to sleep. I just feel worn out most of the time.\n\nDoctor: For at least six months, have you had these problems with people watching you and sending notes about you?\n\nPatient: Six months? No, it's been longer than that. It's been about a year and a month now, dealing with them watching me and the notes and everything. I know exactly how long because it started right around the time the transmission went out on my old Buick. Just clunked out right in the middle of the intersection over by the hardware store. \n\nThat hardware store's been there since I was a kid, you know. You go in there and it has that specific smell... like sawdust and fertilizer mixed together. They used to have this old golden retriever that just slept by the front door. You'd have to step right over him to get to the paint aisle. Paint's gotten so expensive lately, too. I was looking at a can of white semi-gloss a while back just to touch up the baseboards, and I couldn't believe the price. Used to be you could get a gallon for a few bucks. My dad was a house painter for a little while, back in the seventies. He always wore these white overalls that were just covered in every color imaginable. He looked like a modern art painting himself by the time he got home. He'd leave his boots on the porch so he wouldn't track it in... \n\nBut, uh, yeah. To answer your question, it's been a year and a month since all this stuff with the neighbors started.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I have. I used to look forward to a lot of things, really. Choir practice on Wednesdays, playing bridge with the guys, even just cooking up a big pot of beans on Sunday... I used to do little repair jobs for folks around the building, too, fixing leaky faucets or patching drywall. But now, I just mostly let the phone ring. I tell myself there's no point in going or doing any of it. \n\nI used to be real handy, you know. I had this big red toolbox my brother gave me for my birthday one year. It had all these little compartments on top for screws and nails, kept everything sorted. Sorting things used to be real calming for me. I'd sit at the kitchen table and organize all my hardware by size. My brother, he moved out to Arizona about ten years ago. Said he couldn't take the winters here anymore. He bought a house out in the desert, said he doesn't even have to mow the lawn because it's all rocks and cactus. Sounds kind of nice, not having to worry about yard work. Mowers can be a real hassle when the carburetor gets gunked up. You gotta take the whole thing apart and soak it in carburetor cleaner... smells awful, that stuff. Gives you a headache if you don't do it in a well-ventilated area. But yeah, I just don't have the energy or the care for any of my old hobbies anymore. Just seems pointless.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you still believed your neighbors were watching you and sending notes about you, but you were not feeling depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Yeah, there was. There was a spell of several weeks, maybe a month or so ago, where I was actually eating a bit better. Woke up not feeling quite so... heavy and hopeless, you know? But I still knew they were watching my door. Still knew they were reporting on me. \n\nSpeaking of doors, my front door has this squeak that just won't quit. It's the bottom hinge. I've tried WD-40, I've tried rubbing a bar of soap on it—my mom used to swear by that trick for squeaky floorboards. She had this old house with these original hardwood floors that groaned every time you took a step. It was impossible to sneak down to the kitchen for a midnight snack when I was a kid. She'd always catch me. She'd be sitting there in the dark, drinking a glass of warm milk. Said it helped her sleep. I never understood how people could drink warm milk, it just coats your throat in this weird way. But she liked it. She always had it in this chipped blue mug with a little white sailboat painted on it. I wonder whatever happened to that mug... probably got lost in the shuffle when we cleaned out her house. It's funny the things you hold onto and the things that just slip away. But yeah, even when I was feeling a bit more normal, eating alright and not so down, I still knew they had their eyes on me and were sending those notes.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that outside forces were controlling your actions?\n\nPatient: Outside forces? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I'm the one moving my own arms and legs, you know? I do what I decide to do. \n\nSpeaking of legs, though, my left knee has been acting up lately whenever it rains. Just aches right down to the bone. Reminds me of when I fell off the roof of my buddy Al's garage back in '88. We were trying to fix the shingles before a big storm rolled in. Black clouds just sitting over the whole neighborhood, looked like the end of the world. Al had this old Ford pickup truck parked in the driveway, a real beauty, cherry red. He used to spend every Saturday polishing that thing until you could see your reflection in the bumper. Chrome bumpers... they don't make 'em like that anymore. Now everything's just plastic and fiberglass on cars. If you get into a fender bender nowadays, the whole front end just crumbles like a soda can. Used to be you could just take a rubber mallet and pound a dent right out yourself. I had a really good rubber mallet, actually... heavy wooden handle. Left it in the trunk of that Buick I was telling you about earlier. Shame to lose a good tool.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, not really other things. It's just... I came in here because I know the people at my apartment are watching my comings and goings. They're slipping notes about me to the office. Everybody keeps telling me it's just regular notices on the doors, like for pest control or whatever, but I know what they're really doing. 

Pest control, though... they came by a few months ago spraying for roaches. Used this stuff that smelled like burnt tires. Reminded me of when I worked at the junkyard for a bit, back in my twenties. We'd have these huge piles of tires, mountains of them. Sometimes in the summer, the heat would just bake the rubber and the smell would hang over the whole lot. We had this big guard dog there, a Rottweiler mix named Buster. Meanest looking dog you ever saw, but he was a total softie if you had a piece of beef jerky. I used to buy those long sticks of jerky from the gas station on the corner. The guy who ran the place, Sal, he always gave me a discount because I helped him fix his cash register once. It was one of those old mechanical ones with the heavy keys that make a real loud *cha-ching* sound. You don't hear that sound anymore. Now it's all just beeps and touchscreens. You can't even feel the buttons. I like to feel a button when I press it, you know? Makes you feel like you've actually done something. But yeah, everybody says it's just notices on the doors, but I know they're keeping track of me.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, nice to meet you too. I came in because... well, it's the people at my apartment. I know they're watching my comings and goings. It's a big brick building, you know, built back in the 70s. My uncle actually used to do bricklaying on places like that. He helped build the old library downtown before they tore it down. Real shame they knocked it down, they had this whole section on old cars I used to look at. \n\nBut anyway... yeah, they're watching me, and they keep slipping notes about me to the front office.
